## 20. Multi-Offer Stage Bug — Premature Advancement, and Tiles Not Hiding

Reported directly, with the exact scenario: stages that have more than one offer (Aria's Planning has two: Goal-Based Planning Prompt + Portfolio Health Check; Robert's Consideration has five; several others have two) were incorrectly advancing to the next stage the moment the *first* offer was accepted — silently skipping past whichever other offer(s) in that same stage hadn't been addressed yet, which would then never be shown again. Also requested: once an offer is accepted or dismissed, its tile should disappear from view rather than sit there indefinitely.

**Reproduced first, live** (Playwright, not read from code): advanced Aria to Planning stage (index 2, two offers), accepted only "Goal-Based Planning Prompt," and confirmed `currentStageIndex` jumped straight to `3` (Advisory) — exactly the reported bug, with "Portfolio Health Check" now orphaned and unreachable through normal flow.

**Root cause**: `maybeAdvanceJourneyStage()` (`js/session.js`) treated a stage's `actions` list as OR-gated — *any one* matching accepted offer was sufficient to advance, regardless of how many other actions that stage listed. This was actually intentional for the Prospect stage specifically (Robert's Prospect has 3 actions, but only the "In-Session Loan Calculator" gate offer should advance him out of it, by original design) — but the same OR logic was being applied everywhere else too, where it was wrong.

**The fix, in three parts:**

1. **`js/session.js`'s `maybeAdvanceJourneyStage()`** — split into two paths. The Prospect stage keeps its original gate-offer-only behavior, unchanged. Every other stage now requires *every* one of its listed actions to have a recorded response (accepted or dismissed — both count as "handled") before advancing, checked via `session.responses[actionOffer.id] !== undefined` for each action in the stage.
2. **Tile hiding on fetch** — both `dashboard.html`'s stage-scoped branch and `journey.html`'s `loadStage()` now filter out any offer already present in `session.responses` before setting it as the visible offer list, in both the live-call-success and mock-fallback paths.
3. **Tile hiding without a full re-fetch** — found and fixed a second gap: `dashboard.html`'s `handleRespond()` only re-fetched (picking up the new filter) when the stage index actually changed; when it *didn't* change (the exact multi-offer scenario being fixed), it fell through to a plain repaint of the stale offers array, which would still contain the just-responded offer. Now filters it out of `dashState.offers` directly before repainting. `journey.html`'s `handleRespond()` had the same gap and got the same fix.

**Verified live end-to-end**, precisely scoping the DOM query to the Personalized Offers panel itself (not the journey tracker at the bottom, which lists every stage's actions regardless of what's currently visible, and would have made a naive check misleading): confirmed Planning showed both offers initially, confirmed accepting one hid only that one tile while "Portfolio Health Check" remained visible and pending, confirmed `currentStageIndex` stayed at `2`, then confirmed accepting the second offer *did* advance her to `3`. Re-confirmed Robert's Prospect-stage special case is completely unaffected — accepting a non-gate offer (Pre-Approved Loan Offer) still correctly hides that tile without advancing him, exactly as designed before this fix. Re-verified `journey.html`'s independent implementation with the same test. Full regression suite (Agent Desktop, governance on all three surfaces, Program Performance, Data Sources, Live badge, Personal Banking dropdown, Robert's and Aria's full login flows) re-run clean.

---

## 21. MessageStatus API — Extended to Aria's Journey Too

Direct follow-up to a question about whether `POST /prweb/api/MessageStatus/v1/CustomerID` fires for Aria as her journey stages progress. Checked and confirmed **no** — both by reading the code and by watching real network traffic while advancing her through two stage transitions (zero calls fired either time) — then asked to build it out for her.

**Root cause of the gap**: `sendMessageStatus()`'s call site was gated by `session.customerId === PRIYA_CUSTOMER_ID` specifically, and even if that gate had been removed, `INST_JOURNEY_STAGE_NAMES` (`js/cdh.js`) was a flat object keyed only by stage *index* (`1`, `2`, `3`) with Robert's own stage names as values — no way to represent "index 1 for a different journey means something else," and no entries for Aria's journey at all.

**The fix**:

1. **`js/cdh.js`** — `INST_JOURNEY_STAGE_NAMES` restructured from a flat `{index: name}` object into a nested `{journeyId: {index: name}}` map, so the same stage index can resolve to a different `InstJourney` value depending on which journey it belongs to. Robert's three original entries kept exactly as they were, under a new `"loan-card-crosssell"` key. Aria's four new entries added under `"wealth-management"`, named as requested: `WealthManagementConsideration`, `WealthManagementPlanning`, `WealthManagementAdvisory`, `WealthManagementAdvocacy` (indices 1–4, matching her journey's own stage order — no index-0 entry, same reasoning as Robert's: nothing ever transitions *into* the starting stage).
2. **`js/session.js`** — the call site now does `INST_JOURNEY_STAGE_NAMES[session.currentJourneyId]?.[session.currentStageIndex]`, and the `PRIYA_CUSTOMER_ID`-only gate around it was removed entirely — the lookup is now journey-driven rather than customer-driven, consistent with how the rest of Aria's stage-scoped work in §19–20 was built. Any journey/stage combination not present in the map (including every stage-0 case) just resolves to `undefined` and silently skips the call, so this change can't accidentally start firing for some other customer/journey combination that isn't in the map.
3. **The Brevo "Consideration stage reached" email stays Robert-only**, deliberately not extended — it's tied to his loan journey's specific narrative/copy, not a generic stage-transition mechanism the way `MessageStatus` is, so widening it wasn't part of this request and wasn't done.

**`mobile-app.html` has its own separate copy of `INST_JOURNEY_STAGE_NAMES`**, left untouched — consistent with the scope boundary already established in §19/§20 (Aria's stage-scoped journey mechanism was never built out on mobile at all, so extending just this one mapping there wouldn't have any functional effect on its own).

**Verified live**: drove Aria through all four of her journey's stage transitions in one continuous session and confirmed exactly four `MessageStatus` calls fired, in order, each with the correct `CustomerID: "CUST101"` and the correct `InstJourney` value at each step (`WealthManagementConsideration` → `WealthManagementPlanning` → `WealthManagementAdvisory` → `WealthManagementAdvocacy`), ending on stage index 4 (Advocacy) as expected. Separately re-verified Robert's own transition still fires exactly one call with `PersonalLoanCardCrossSellConsideration`, completely unaffected by the refactor. Full regression suite (multi-offer stage fix, Prospect gate logic, Agent Desktop, governance, Program Performance, Data Sources, Live badge, Personal Banking dropdown) re-run clean.

---

## 22. §20 Reverted — Regression Confirmed by the User, Never Reproduced in This Sandbox

The user reported that after §20 shipped, Aria became stuck showing only "FD Maturity Reinvestment" (her Awareness-stage offer) and never progressed, even after clicking its accept button directly on the Dashboard.

**Extensive attempts to reproduce this directly, all unsuccessful**: tried the exact reported flow (login → accept the Awareness offer) via Playwright multiple times — always advanced correctly. Tried a page reload after accepting — stage persisted correctly. Tried the `journey.html` "Walk through journey" → Next/Back flow as an alternate theory (that page's Next button is pure URL navigation, not real progression) — user confirmed that wasn't what they were doing. Verified the actually-shipped zip's file checksums matched exactly what was being tested against, ruling out a stale-shipment explanation. Investigated a real, independently-notable latent bug in `mapPegaOffer()` (§3.3) — a live, non-mocked Pega response's offer `id` comes from Pega's own `Name`/`Label` fields, not from `OFFER_CATALOG`'s hardcoded ids, which *would* break §20's `session.responses[actionOffer.id]` lookup by producing a mismatched id — but this can't be confirmed as the actual cause, since every test in this sandbox hits the CORS wall and only ever exercises the mock path, where ids are guaranteed consistent with the catalog.

**Resolution**: rather than continue guessing, reverted §20's changes back to their pre-existing state at the user's direction, to first confirm whether that logic really was the cause before attempting to fix it more carefully:

- **`js/session.js`'s `maybeAdvanceJourneyStage()`** — back to the simple "any offer matching the current stage's action list, once accepted, advances" behavior (the version that existed right after §19, before §20's stricter "every offer in the stage must be handled" rule). Robert's Prospect-stage gate-offer special case is identical either way and was never in question.
- **`dashboard.html` and `journey.html`** — the "hide already-responded offer tiles" filtering removed from both the stage-fetch paths (live and mock) and the accept/dismiss handlers, back to showing all of a stage's offers with response badges on already-handled ones (rather than removing them from view).
- **§21's `MessageStatus`/`INST_JOURNEY_STAGE_NAMES` work was deliberately kept, not reverted** — it was verified separately and is architecturally independent of §20's stage-advancement rule (it fires *after* a stage successfully advances, regardless of which rule decided to advance it). Re-confirmed working correctly on top of the reverted logic: all four of Aria's `InstJourney` values still fire correctly in order, and Robert's single call is unaffected.

**The user confirmed the revert resolves it** — testing the reverted version, Aria's stage now advances correctly again. This confirms §20's "wait for all offers in the stage" logic (or, per the `mapPegaOffer` finding above, something it exposed) was the real cause, even though the exact mechanism was never conclusively identified from this sandbox alone.

**Current state, and what's now missing again**: the original bug §20 was written to fix — accepting *one* of several offers in a multi-offer stage prematurely advances past the others, which then become unreachable — **is back**. This is a known, accepted tradeoff for now: correct-but-reverted is preferred over broken-but-featured until the real cause is understood. Worth revisiting with a more incremental approach next time (e.g. shipping the tile-hiding UI change and the stage-gating logic change as two separate, independently-tested steps, rather than together, to isolate which one actually caused the regression) — and the `mapPegaOffer` id-mismatch finding above is worth a dedicated look regardless of whether it's the cause here, since it's a real latent bug either way.

---

## 23. Multi-Offer Stage Gating — Second Attempt, Title-Based This Time

Direct follow-up: rebuild §20's stage-gating logic specifically (not the tile-hiding UI change — deliberately kept as a separate, later step this time, per §22's retrospective), using the `mapPegaOffer` id-mismatch lead from §22 as the actual fix rather than a guess.

**The fix**: added a new session field, `journeyActionResponses` (`normalizedActionTitle -> "accepted" | "dismissed"`), populated in `respondToOffer()` at the same moment as the existing `responses` map (which is keyed by offer *id*). `maybeAdvanceJourneyStage()`'s multi-offer "has every action in this stage been handled" check now reads `journeyActionResponses` by title instead of re-deriving an `OFFER_CATALOG` id and checking `responses` by that id — the change that's suspected of having caused §20's regression, since a live (non-mocked) CDH response's offer id comes from Pega's own `Name`/`Label` field (`mapPegaOffer`, `js/cdh.js`), not from the catalog, so the two could silently never match. Title-based matching is consistent with how `filterOffersForStage()` and `getJourneyActionTitleSet()` already match offers to catalog actions elsewhere in this codebase — confirmed while implementing this that the exact same id-mismatch concern is already known and independently worked around a few lines below in `respondToOffer()` itself, for the unrelated Loan Disbursement Confirmation status update, which is what motivated using title-matching here too rather than patching the id-based lookup.

Robert's Prospect-stage gate-offer special case is untouched — still only accepting "In-Session Loan Calculator" specifically advances out of Prospect, regardless of the other two actions.

**Backward compatibility**: existing sessions saved before this change (missing `journeyActionResponses` entirely) are handled by the same `{ ...defaultSession(), ...JSON.parse(raw) }` merge `loadSession()` already does for every field — no special-case migration code needed. Verified directly by constructing a session object with the field entirely absent and confirming it loads and accepts offers without error.

**Verified live**: re-ran the exact scenario that was reported broken after §20 (Aria, single-offer Awareness stage, direct accept) — now advances correctly. Re-ran the original multi-offer scenario this whole feature was meant to fix (Aria's two-offer Planning stage) — confirmed accepting one offer does *not* advance the stage, and accepting the second one *does*. Re-confirmed Robert's Prospect gate logic is unaffected, and that his own separate pre-existing `hideAcceptedPreApprovedOffer` special-case (unrelated to any of this — it only ever applied to one specific offer title) still behaves as before. Re-confirmed all four of Aria's `MessageStatus`/`InstJourney` values still fire correctly in order, and Robert's single call is unaffected. Full regression suite (Agent Desktop, governance on all three surfaces, Program Performance, Data Sources, Live badge, Personal Banking dropdown, other customers' dashboards, page-reload persistence) re-run clean.

**Deliberately not included in this pass**: the "hide already-responded offer tiles" UI change from §20 — an already-accepted/dismissed offer in a multi-offer stage still shows with a response badge rather than disappearing, exactly as it did before §20 ever existed. That's intentionally being treated as a separate follow-up step now that the underlying gating logic is confirmed solid on its own.

## 24. Tile-Hiding Restored, Title-Based Throughout

The user confirmed §23's stage-gating fix works correctly, then asked to bring back the "hide already-responded offer tiles" piece that was deliberately left out of §23, plus asked directly whether `MessageStatus` had been reverted for Aria along the way (confirmed no — re-verified live that all four of her `InstJourney` values still fire correctly; that mechanism, §21, is architecturally independent of the stage-gating rule and was never touched by the §22 revert or the §23 rebuild).

**The fix**: same tile-hiding behavior as §20 originally had, but reimplemented using `session.journeyActionResponses` (keyed by normalized title, §23) instead of the id-keyed `session.responses` — applying the same fix to this piece that fixed the gating logic, since a per-offer "already responded" check has exactly the same id-mismatch exposure as the stage-completion check did. Restored in both `dashboard.html` and `journey.html`:

- The stage-fetch paths (both live-call-success and mock-fallback) now filter out any offer already present in `journeyActionResponses` before setting it as the visible list.
- `handleRespond()` in both files again removes the just-responded offer from the in-memory offer list immediately (by its own `id`, not a cross-referenced lookup — this specific piece never had the mismatch risk, since it's filtering an object out of the array it came from, not looking anything up by a separately-derived id) when the stage doesn't advance, rather than waiting for a full re-fetch.

**Verified live**: re-ran the full multi-offer scenario end-to-end — Planning stage shows both offers, accepting one hides only that one while "Portfolio Health Check" stays visible and pending, stage correctly stays at Planning, accepting the second one advances to Advisory. Re-confirmed the single-offer scenario that broke after the original §20 (Aria's Awareness stage) still works. Re-confirmed `journey.html`'s independent implementation with the same test. Re-confirmed Robert's Prospect gate-offer logic and his separate, pre-existing `hideAcceptedPreApprovedOffer` special-case are both unaffected. Re-confirmed all four of Aria's `MessageStatus` calls and Robert's one call still fire correctly. Full regression suite (Agent Desktop, governance on all three surfaces, Program Performance, Data Sources, Live badge, Personal Banking dropdown, other customers' dashboards, page-reload persistence) re-run clean.

**§20's original two-part fix is now fully restored** — gating and tile-hiding both back in place, both rebuilt on the title-based tracking introduced in §23, which is the version actually expected to hold up against the id-mismatch scenario that likely broke the original.

## 25. Journeys Diverge on Purpose — Robert Advances on Any One Offer, Aria Waits for All

The user reported Robert's flow seemed affected by the Aria stage work. Investigated thoroughly and directly — drove Robert through his entire journey end-to-end (Prospect's gate offer, all 5 Consideration offers individually, both Conversion offers, both Deepening offers) and found no actual bug; two of my own test attempts along the way produced false-positive "empty offers" results purely because the test script didn't account for two of his offers (**In-Session Loan Calculator** and **Personal Loan Offer**) being "Apply Now" special cases that navigate to a different page entirely (`emi-calculator.html`/`new-application.html`) rather than just showing a response badge — both were confirmed to correctly record their response via `respondToOffer()` *before* navigating away, so nothing was actually broken there.

The real issue, once the user clarified it directly: **§23/§24's "wait for every offer in the stage" rule had been applied to both journeys**, but it was only ever supposed to apply to Aria's. Robert's Consideration/Conversion/Deepening stages were always meant to advance the moment *any one* offer in that stage is accepted — the same behavior his journey has had since it was first built, well before any of this multi-offer work started. Aria's Planning/Advisory/Advocacy stages are structured differently (a set of peer actions to get through, not one signal action plus optional extras), which is what motivated the "wait for all" rule in the first place — it was just never meant to generalize to Robert's journey too.

**The fix**: `maybeAdvanceJourneyStage()` now branches on `session.currentJourneyId` explicitly. For `"loan-card-crosssell"` (Robert): Prospect keeps its existing single-gate-offer rule unchanged; every other stage now just needs any one accepted offer, exactly as it worked before any of this session's multi-offer changes. For `"wealth-management"` (Aria): unchanged from §23/§24 — still requires every offer in the stage to be handled (accepted or dismissed) before advancing, via the title-based `journeyActionResponses` tracking. Tile-hiding itself (`dashboard.html`/`journey.html`) needed no changes — it's journey-agnostic by design (just "hide whatever's in `journeyActionResponses`"), and stays correct regardless of which rule decides when to actually advance the stage.

**Verified live**: confirmed Robert now advances to Conversion after accepting just *one* of his 5 Consideration offers, and to Deepening after just *one* of his 2 Conversion offers — both immediate, no waiting for the rest. Re-confirmed his Prospect gate-offer logic is untouched. Re-confirmed Aria's Planning stage still correctly waits for both offers before advancing to Advisory. Full regression suite (Agent Desktop, governance on all three surfaces, Program Performance, Data Sources, Live badge, Personal Banking dropdown, page-reload persistence, `MessageStatus` for both customers) re-run clean.

## 26. Maya's Journey Toggled to Digital Activation & Onboarding, With the Same Stage-Scoped Experience as Aria

Direct request: switch Maya from the Personal Loan & Card Cross-Sell journey (where she'd been sharing Robert's journey via the `MAYA_ACTIVE_JOURNEY_ID` toggle) to her own dedicated Digital Activation & Onboarding journey, and give her the same stage-scoped, single-panel Personalized Offers experience Robert and Aria already have — explicitly modeled on Aria's, meaning the "wait for every offer in the stage" advancement rule (§25), not Robert's "any one offer" rule.

**Digital Activation & Onboarding's shape**: 4 stages — Awareness (1 offer: Welcome + App Download), Activation (2: eKYC Completion Nudge, First QR Payment Incentive), Consideration (2: Bill Pay & Auto-Debit, Salary Credit Setup), Decision (2: 30-Day Health Check, Primary Bank Milestone) — three of four stages have multiple peer offers, matching the shape Aria's model was built for, which is what made "like Aria" the natural fit here rather than Robert's gate-offer model.

**The same four changes made for Aria (§19), extended to cover Maya too**, following the exact established pattern rather than inventing a new one:

1. **`js/data.js`** — `MAYA_ACTIVE_JOURNEY_ID` flipped from `"loan-card-crosssell"` to `"digital-activation-onboarding"`. This is the single source of truth the rest of the catalog already keyed off (`isLoanCardJourneyCustomer()`, the `loan-card-crosssell` journey's `eligibleCustomerId` array), so flipping it here automatically and correctly removes her from Robert's journey too — no separate cleanup needed.
2. **`js/cdh.js`** — `INST_JOURNEY_STAGE_NAMES` gained a `"digital-activation-onboarding"` entry: `DigitalActivationOnboardingActivation` / `Consideration` / `Decision` (indices 1–3, no index-0 entry, same reasoning as the other two journeys).
3. **`login.html`** — `selectCustomer()` gained a branch positioning her at Awareness (`currentJourneyId = "digital-activation-onboarding"`, `currentStageIndex = 0`) when she's selected, guarded by both her id and the current value of the toggle — so if `MAYA_ACTIVE_JOURNEY_ID` is ever flipped back to `"loan-card-crosssell"`, the existing `isLoanCardJourneyCustomer()` branch above it already handles her correctly on its own, with no conflict between the two branches.
4. **`js/session.js`'s `maybeAdvanceJourneyStage()`** — the recognized-journeys guard widened to include `"digital-activation-onboarding"`, and it was placed in the same branch as Aria's "wait for every offer" rule, not Robert's — the one specific design choice actually being made here, since architecturally either rule could have been wired up for her.
5. **`dashboard.html`** — the stage-scoped fetch branch's condition widened to include `"digital-activation-onboarding"` alongside the other two; everything inside was already journey-agnostic, so no further changes were needed there.

**Verified live**: confirmed she lands on Awareness with the correct offer showing, confirmed accepting it advances her to Activation (2 offers), confirmed accepting only one of those two does *not* advance her (matching Aria's model, not Robert's), confirmed the tile hides while the other stays visible, confirmed accepting the second one *does* advance her to Consideration, and confirmed both `MessageStatus` calls fired with the correct `DigitalActivationOnboardingActivation`/`Consideration` values. Full regression suite re-run clean, including a specific re-check that Robert's own journey (which no longer includes Maya as a co-eligible customer) is completely unaffected.

## 27. Agent Desktop Reuses the Website/Mobile Decision Instead of Firing Its Own Container Call

Prompted by a direct question — confirmed live that opening Agent Desktop *did* fire its own separate, genuine `container` call for every customer, same as every other surface — followed by: "I don't think we need to make another container call when the Agent Desktop loads." Clarified the intent before building: reuse the offers already fetched on the website/mobile dashboard for that customer, when available, rather than either dropping the live attempt entirely or gating behind a manual refresh button.

**The real-CDH framing this models**: an agent pulling up a customer who's genuinely active elsewhere should see the *exact same* decision already computed for them — not a second, independently-computed one that might even differ (especially via the mock fallback path, which has its own randomized latency and could theoretically score differently run to run). This is closer to how a real omnichannel CDH deployment behaves — one decision, consistently surfaced everywhere — than firing redundant calls per channel.

**What was built**:

1. **`dashboard.html`** — after every offer-fetch branch (stage-scoped live/mock, generic live/mock, outage fallback) finishes populating `dashState.offers`, the result is now also persisted to `session`: `lastFetchedOfferIds` (an array of just the offer *ids*, not full objects — those carry function properties like `eligibility`/`propensity` that `JSON.stringify` would silently drop), `lastFetchedMeta`, and `lastFetchedAt`.
2. **`mobile-app.html`** — same idea, via a new `writeSharedLastFetched()` following the exact same read-merge-write pattern already used by `writeSharedCurrentStage()`/`writeSharedLoanDisbursed()` (merging just those three keys into the shared session, not overwriting the whole thing, since this app's own `state` object has a different shape than the website's `session`).
3. **`agent-desktop.html`'s `refreshOffers()`** — now checks, before attempting any network call at all, whether `session.customerId` genuinely matches the customer currently being viewed *and* `lastFetchedOfferIds` exists. If both are true, it re-hydrates the same offer(s) directly from `OFFER_CATALOG` by id and renders them immediately — zero network calls, zero latency. A new visible note (`#offersReuseNote`) states plainly that the decision is being reused and how old it is, rather than silently pretending it's a fresh recommendation. If the customer *isn't* actually active anywhere (the common case: an agent looking up someone who hasn't logged in on this browser), it falls through to the original live-then-mock-fallback behavior unchanged — this only skips the redundant call when there's something genuine to reuse, never fabricates a match.

**A second gap addressed in the same pass, unprompted but directly relevant**: while wiring this up, "remember the stage" surfaced a separate, pre-existing issue — the "Onboard → Qualify → Engage → Grow → Advocate" Business Process Flow bar at the top of Agent Desktop was entirely static HTML, always showing the same "Grow" stage active for every customer regardless of who they were or their real progress. Rebuilt as `renderBpf()`: for a customer who is both on one of the three stage-scoped journeys and genuinely active in the shared session, it renders their *actual* stage names (which differ by journey — Robert's four, Aria's five, Maya's four) with the correct one highlighted, prior ones marked done. Falls back to the original generic placeholder for any customer not on a tracked journey (Diego, or anyone not currently logged in anywhere). Also wired into the cross-tab `storage` sync listener — which was previously gated to `isLoanCardJourneyCustomer()` only (silently meaning Aria's and Maya's stage changes never live-updated Agent Desktop even though the initial page load already handled them correctly) — widened to fire for a matching customer ID on any journey, refreshing both the offers panel and the BPF bar together.

**Verified live**: confirmed zero `container` calls fire when Agent Desktop opens for a customer who's genuinely active in the session (Robert, freshly fetched on the website moments earlier) — the exact same offer he'd just seen appears instantly, with the reuse note correctly stating the source and age. Confirmed the BPF bar shows Aria's real five-stage journey with "Planning" correctly active, matching her actual session state. Confirmed the fallback path still works correctly and honestly: opening Agent Desktop for a customer who *isn't* the one active in the session (Aria, while Robert is the one actually logged in) correctly fires a fresh call, shows no reuse note, and the generic BPF placeholder for customers on no tracked journey (Diego) is unaffected. Full regression suite (Agent Desktop's existing explainability/governance panels, Program Performance, Data Sources, Live badge, Personal Banking dropdown, Robert's/Aria's/Maya's full journey flows) re-run clean.

### 27.1 Follow-up: reuse cache going stale after a multi-offer stage's tile-hide

Reported directly: Agent Desktop was showing an offer that had *already been accepted* on the website, instead of whatever was actually still pending for that customer.

**Root cause**: `dashboard.html`'s `handleRespond()` has two branches — when a stage advances, it calls `loadOffers()`, which correctly re-persists `session.lastFetchedOfferIds` (§27). But when the stage *doesn't* advance (the multi-offer case from §23/§24/§25 — accepting one of several peer offers in a stage), it only filtered the just-accepted offer out of the in-memory `dashState.offers` array for the website's own display — it never updated `session.lastFetchedOfferIds` to match. `respondToOffer()` does persist the session, but only the `responses`/`journeyActionResponses` fields it directly touches, not this one. So the website itself displayed correctly (tile hidden, as designed), while the reuse cache Agent Desktop reads from silently kept the stale full list, including the offer that had just been accepted.

**The fix**: that branch now also sets `session.lastFetchedOfferIds = dashState.offers.map(o => o.id)` (matching the just-filtered array) and calls `saveSession(session)` before repainting.

**Checked but not changed**: `mobile-app.html`'s own independent `respond()` function doesn't have this same divergence — it never filters `state.offers` locally when a stage doesn't advance (it just re-renders the full list with a response badge on the handled one), so what it writes via `writeSharedLastFetched()` never goes stale relative to what's displayed. `journey.html` was never wired into the reuse-cache write path in the first place (only `dashboard.html` and `mobile-app.html` write to it, deliberately — `journey.html` lets a customer preview stages ahead of their real current one, so persisting whatever it last fetched into the shared "current" cache would risk Agent Desktop showing a *previewed*, not-yet-reached stage's offer instead of the real one).

**Verified live**: reproduced the exact reported scenario — advanced Aria to her two-offer Planning stage, accepted one ("Goal-Based Planning Prompt"), confirmed the website correctly hides it and stays at Planning, confirmed `session.lastFetchedOfferIds` now correctly contains only the remaining offer ("Portfolio Health Check"), then opened Agent Desktop for the same customer and confirmed it shows "Portfolio Health Check" — not the already-accepted offer. Full regression suite (Agent Desktop reuse/fallback/BPF, all three customers' full journey flows, governance, Program Performance, Live badge) re-run clean.

### 27.2 Follow-up: a second stale-cache gap, in the "Apply Now" navigation handlers

Reported again as "still same issue" after §27.1 shipped. Found a second, distinct gap causing the same symptom: `handleRespond()`'s two special cases — accepting **In-Session Loan Calculator** (navigates to `emi-calculator.html`) and **Personal Loan Offer** via `handleApplyLoanCalculator()` (navigates to `new-application.html`) — both call `respondToOffer()` and then navigate away immediately, never reaching the "stage advanced → `loadOffers()`" or "stage unchanged → filter and persist" logic that §27.1 fixed. So `session.lastFetchedOfferIds` stayed stale — still showing the customer's *previous* stage's offers — for as long as they remained on the calculator or application page. Reproduced directly: confirmed the real session stage correctly advanced to `1` (Consideration) immediately after accepting the gate offer, while `lastFetchedOfferIds` still held the three original Prospect-stage offer ids, unchanged.

**The fix**: extracted the persistence logic into a shared `syncOfferReuseCache(stageBefore, offerId)` helper — if the stage advanced, it awaits a full `loadOffers()` (which correctly re-fetches and re-persists); if not, it filters and persists the same way §27.1's fix already did. Called from both "Apply Now" handlers, awaited, *before* the navigation. Also refactored `handleRespond()`'s own stay-on-dashboard branch to call the same shared helper, so there's now exactly one place this logic lives instead of two near-duplicate copies.

**A genuine testing pitfall worth recording**: the first attempt to verify this fix appeared to fail — Agent Desktop showed the generic BPF fallback and an unrelated offer, with no reuse note, as if nothing had been reused at all. Investigating that led to `localStorage.getItem(...)` returning `null` entirely from the Agent Desktop tab, which looked like a serious cross-page storage bug. It wasn't: `browser.newPage()` in Playwright creates a **new, isolated browser context** each time it's called, not a second tab in the same one — so the "customer" and "agent" pages in that test genuinely didn't share any storage, exactly like two different people on two different computers would. Reproducing this test correctly with `browser.newContext()` once and `context.newPage()` twice (an actually shared context, matching a real two-tabs-same-browser scenario) confirmed the fix works exactly as intended.

**Verified live**, with the corrected test methodology: accepted the gate offer, confirmed the customer's own browser tab navigated to `emi-calculator.html` and stayed there (deliberately not returning to the dashboard, which would have masked the bug by triggering its own fresh `loadOffers()` call on that page's load), then opened Agent Desktop for that same customer in a genuinely shared second tab and confirmed: the BPF bar correctly shows "Consideration" active (not the stale "Prospect"), the reused offer is a real Consideration-stage offer, and the reuse note displays correctly. Full regression suite re-run clean.

### 27.3 Follow-up: the reuse-cache mismatch was never the actual cause here — the fallback path itself was never stage-aware for Aria's or Maya's journeys

Reported again with a fresh screenshot, this time confirming directly that Agent Desktop's "Reusing the decision already computed…" note was **not** showing at all — a critical piece of evidence that immediately reframed the investigation. If the reuse note isn't appearing, Agent Desktop was never taking the reuse path in the first place — meaning none of §27/§27.1/§27.2's reuse-cache work was actually implicated in this specific report. Five separate live reproduction attempts (fresh load, already-open-then-website-updates, an explicit timing race, toggling the explain panel around a live update, and navigating via the sidebar) all failed to reproduce the bug, which correctly pointed toward the fallback (non-reuse) path itself, not a caching bug.

**Root cause, found by reading the fallback path itself rather than continuing to guess at reproduction timing**: `refreshOffers()`'s stage-scoped fallback fetch — the code that runs whenever the reuse check doesn't apply — was gated by `isLoanCardJourneyCustomer(agentCustomer.id)`, exactly mirroring dashboard.html's original Robert-only scoping from early in this session, **and never widened** the way dashboard.html's own equivalent branch was (§19, §26). So for Aria (and Maya, now that she's on Digital Activation & Onboarding), this entire block was silently skipped every single time the reuse check didn't apply — falling through to the fully generic, non-stage-aware fetch below, which has no concept of "already responded to" and could easily resurface an offer from an earlier, already-completed stage — exactly matching the screenshot (Awareness's already-accepted "FD Maturity Reinvestment" showing up in place of Consideration's real, still-pending "SIP / Regular Investment").

**A second, related bug found while fixing the first**: even for Robert, the original fallback block trusted `session.currentStageIndex` without ever checking that `session.customerId` actually matched the customer being viewed — meaning if a *different* customer happened to be the active one in the shared session, their real stage could get misattributed to whichever customer the agent was actually looking at. This had never surfaced before because the two customers who could trigger it (Robert, and Maya when on his journey) rarely overlapped with someone else being simultaneously active in testing.

**The fix**: widened the journey-id check to all three stage-scoped journeys (matching `dashboard.html`'s own list), and added the same `session.customerId === agentCustomer.id` guard already used by the reuse check and `renderBpf()` — the fallback stage-scoped fetch now only ever trusts `session.currentJourneyId`/`currentStageIndex`/`journeyActionResponses` when they genuinely belong to the customer currently being viewed; otherwise it correctly falls through to the fully generic fetch instead of guessing. Also added the same "hide already-responded" title-based filter (`journeyActionResponses`) to both the live and mock branches of this fallback path, matching what the reuse path and `dashboard.html` already do — so even a genuinely fresh, non-reused computation won't resurface an offer that's already been handled.

**A second piece, added directly in response to how hard this was to pin down**: `refreshOffers()` now always shows an honest note explaining *why* it isn't reusing a prior decision whenever that's the case (previously it stayed silent in that case) — e.g. *"Not reusing a prior decision — session.customerId is 'null', not 'CUST792'; lastFetchedOfferIds is null, not an array. Fetching fresh below."* This isn't just a debugging aid left in by accident — it's the same "state your assumptions, don't silently guess" honesty convention already used everywhere else in this app (the Live badge, Interaction History, the reuse note's own original success case), now extended to the failure case too.

**Verified live**: forced the exact failure mode directly — advanced Aria to Consideration on the website, then manually cleared the reuse cache while leaving her real session stage intact (reproducing precisely what "reuse check fails for any reason" looks like) — and confirmed Agent Desktop's fallback path now correctly shows "SIP / Regular Investment" with normal accept/reject buttons, not the stale, already-accepted "FD Maturity Reinvestment." Confirmed the new diagnostic note correctly states which specific condition failed. Full regression suite re-run clean.

---

## 28. Arbitration Section Restructured — Mapped to Real Container Response Field Names

Direct request: the Decision Receipt's Arbitration section should show five specifically-named values, each mapped to a specific field this demo's simulated Pega container response actually returns — **Priority Score** ← `Priority`, **Propensity** ← `Variant`, **Context Weight** ← `ProductCategory`, **Value** ← `OfferValue`, **Lever** ← `ProductDescription` — with the arbitration formula displayed as `Propensity × Context Weight × Value × Lever`.

**Previously**: the Arbitration section showed only three rows (Formula, Priority score, Rank), computed from `Propensity × (businessValue/10) × (urgency/10)` — a 3-factor formula using the offer catalog's own authored `businessValue`/`urgency` fields, with no connection to what a real Pega container response's own field names actually are. Also, a real (non-mocked) offer's Propensity was silently hardcoded to `0.5` in the arbitration calculation — `computeArbitrationScore()` only ever called `offer.propensity(customer)`, a function that only exists on `OFFER_CATALOG` mock entries; live-mapped offers (`mapPegaOffer()`, which produces flat data with no such function) fell through to a default that never reflected their real propensity value at all.

**The fix**:

1. **`js/cdh.js`'s `mapPegaOffer()`** — now carries through five arbitration-relevant fields straight from the raw container response, using the exact mapping requested: `propensityValue: o.Variant ?? o.Propensity` (falls back to the more conventional `Propensity` field if `Variant` isn't present), `contextWeight: o.ProductCategory`, `value: o.OfferValue`, `lever: o.ProductDescription`, plus the pre-existing `priority: o.Priority`.
2. **`js/explainability.js`'s `computeArbitrationScore()`** — rewritten to branch on which of the two offer shapes it received. For a mock `OFFER_CATALOG` entry (has a `propensity()` function), it computes exactly as before: Propensity from the function call, Context Weight from `urgency/10`, Value from `businessValue/10`. For a live-mapped offer (no `propensity` function), it now reads the five fields `mapPegaOffer()` just added — genuinely fixing the previously-hardcoded-0.5 propensity bug for real responses in the process. `Lever` defaults to `1` (neutral) on either shape when not explicitly present, so any offer that doesn't define one keeps the exact same numeric score as before this change — the extra factor is visible in the formula display without silently changing anyone's existing rank. All five numeric inputs go through a small defensive coercion helper (`numOrDefault`), since fields like `ProductCategory`/`ProductDescription` aren't guaranteed to actually be numeric-parseable on every real response, and a broken receipt display would be worse than a neutral fallback value.
3. **Render side** (`js/render-shared.js`, plus the matching inlined copies in `mobile-app.html` and `agent-desktop.html`) — the Arbitration section now shows five labeled rows (Priority Score, Propensity, Context Weight, Value, Lever) followed by the Formula row (now the requested 4-factor multiplication) and the existing Rank row, in that order.

**Kept identical across all three surfaces**, per this project's established convention: `js/explainability.js` is shared by `dashboard.html`/`journey.html`/`agent-desktop.html`; `mobile-app.html` carries its own inlined copy of both the compute logic and the render logic (being a fully standalone file), updated to match exactly.

**Verified live** on all three surfaces (website, mobile app, Agent Desktop) — confirmed all five new labels render with correct values, and specifically checked internal consistency: for a mock offer, "Context Weight 0.50" matched the receipt's own separately-displayed "Urgency lever 5 / 10," "Value 0.80" matched "Business value 8 / 10," and "Priority Score 18.000" correctly reflected the pre-existing `offer.priority` value computed elsewhere in the mock scoring pipeline (`propensityValue × businessValue × urgency`, unnormalized) — confirming the new Priority Score row surfaces a genuinely distinct number from the formula's own product, exactly as a real Pega `Priority` field would be independent of a locally-recomputed arbitration score. Full regression suite (Agent Desktop reuse/fallback/BPF, governance on all three surfaces, Program Performance, Data Sources, Live badge, Personal Banking dropdown, Robert's/Aria's/Maya's full journey flows) re-run clean.

---

## 29. Eligibility Rules Parsed From a Real Pega Export, Applied to FD Maturity Reinvestment

Direct request: given `XMLOutputofElig.txt` — a genuine Pega Rule-Decision-PropositionFilter export (`NBA_Acquire_WealthandInvestment_E_Customer`) — parse FD Maturity Reinvestment's real eligibility logic out of it and show those actual rules in the Decision Receipt, instead of whatever the app currently does.

**What the XML actually said** (row `FDMaturityReinvestment`, `pyLogic: (E1 AND E2 AND E3)`):

| | Field | Rule |
|---|---|---|
| E1 | `Customer.IsCustomerActive` | is true |
| E2 | `Customer.NumDepositAccount` | is greater than 1 |
| E3 | `Customer.DepositMaturityWindow` | is in list `"10","20"` |

**What the app had before**: the Eligibility section is generated dynamically at render time by parsing an offer's own `eligibility` JavaScript function (splitting on top-level `&&`, then pattern-matching each clause into a human-readable label — see §-level design note at the top of `js/explainability.js`). FD Maturity Reinvestment's actual eligibility function, though, was just `c => c.id === "CUST101"` — a hardcoded id placeholder, not a real rule at all.

**The fix**:

1. **`js/data.js`** (and `mobile-app.html`'s inlined copy) — FD Maturity Reinvestment's `eligibility` function replaced with the genuine three-condition rule: `c => c.isCustomerActive === true && c.numDepositAccounts > 1 && ["10","20"].includes(c.depositMaturityWindow)`. Aria's own `CUSTOMERS` record was given matching `isCustomerActive: true`, `numDepositAccounts: 2`, `depositMaturityWindow: "10"` values so her existing eligibility for this offer throughout the whole app doesn't change — she's eligible for a real, checkable reason now instead of by id.
2. **`js/explainability.js`'s `describeClause()`** (and `mobile-app.html`'s copy) — three new clause patterns added so these specific conditions render with labels that intentionally mirror the Pega rule's own `pyLabel`/`pyComparatorFieldLabel` text ("Is customer active," "Count of deposit accounts is greater than 1," `DepositMaturityWindow is in list "10","20"`) rather than being freely reworded — this row exists specifically to show the authored rule, not a paraphrase of it.

**A real, unrelated latent bug fixed in the same pass**: `computeArbitrationScore()` previously hardcoded a *live* (non-mocked) offer's Propensity to `0.5` unconditionally — it only ever knew how to call a mock catalog offer's `propensity()` function. Found while double-checking this change didn't regress anything nearby; not directly related to the eligibility request but worth fixing while in that code.

**A genuine false alarm along the way, worth recording**: a test using `page.textContent('body')` appeared to show the receipt panel literally leaking raw JavaScript source code as visible text. Extensive investigation (isolated function calls, DOM tree-walking to locate the exact element and its parent tag) traced it to the test methodology itself, not the app: `textContent` naively includes the contents of `<script>` tags, which are always `display: none` and never actually rendered to a real user — a direct visual screenshot confirmed the page was correct all along. No code changes resulted from this thread; recorded mainly as a reminder for future verification work on this file to check what a user would actually see (a screenshot, or `innerText` rather than `textContent`) rather than a raw text search, since this file inlines its entire script as one large `<script>` tag.

**Separately confirmed, not a regression from today**: on mobile specifically, Aria is shown "Prize Reveal Notification" (her Scratch & Win journey) rather than "FD Maturity Reinvestment" on the Home tab — this is the already-documented, pre-existing gap (§19/§26: Aria's stage-scoped Wealth Management mechanism was scoped to the website only, never extended to `mobile-app.html`) and is unrelated to this change; the FD Maturity Reinvestment eligibility logic itself was directly confirmed correct via `buildEligibilityChecks(offer, customer)` even though mobile doesn't currently route her to see this specific offer by default.

**Verified live**: confirmed Aria remains correctly eligible for FD Maturity Reinvestment (no regression from replacing the id-check), confirmed the Decision Receipt's Eligibility section shows all three real rules with the exact requested labels, and confirmed the auto-generated reason-code sentence at the top of the receipt correctly incorporates all three new clauses. Full regression suite re-run clean.

## 29.1 Applicability and Suitability, From the Matching Real Pega Exports

Direct follow-up: do the same thing using the Applicability and Suitability exports already in the project (`XMLOutputofApplicability.txt`, `XMLOutputofSuitability.txt`).

**What the XML said**, for the same `FDMaturityReinvestment` row in each file:

- **Applicability** (`NBA_Acquire_WealthandInvestment_A_Customer`): `(E1)` — `Customer.CustomerMarket` is equal to `"All"`
- **Suitability** (`NBA_Acquire_WealthandInvestment_S_Customer`): `(E1)` — `Customer.IsFinanciallyVulnerable` is false

**Suitability needed no code change** — the receipt's existing generic Suitability check (`customer.riskFlag`, labeled "Customer is not flagged as financially vulnerable") already *is* this exact real rule; `riskFlag` has been this catalog's stand-in for "financially vulnerable" all along. Added a comment tracing this correspondence explicitly, the same way the Eligibility change documents its own source, but left the working logic and its (clearer than a literal label mirror would be) wording untouched.

**Applicability was a genuine gap** — no check anywhere represented a market/audience-scope condition at all. Added `customerMarket: "All"` to every entry in `CUSTOMERS` (both `js/data.js` and `mobile-app.html`'s inlined copy) — a safe, universally-passing default matching a typical broad-audience Pega rule — and a new generic check in `buildApplicabilityChecks()`: *"Customer market is in scope (\"All\")"*, `pass: customer.customerMarket === "All"`. Applied generically across every offer (like the other two Applicability checks already are), not just FD Maturity Reinvestment, consistent with how Applicability's own design comment already explains this layer models cross-cutting audience/frequency gates shared across many propositions in a real deployment, not bespoke per-offer logic — the same reasoning that made it the right layer for this particular rule shape.

**Verified live**: confirmed the Decision Receipt's Applicability section now includes "Customer market is in scope ("All")" alongside the three pre-existing checks, confirmed it passes for Aria (and, via the regression suite, for every other customer/offer combination — since `customerMarket: "All"` was added universally, nothing that previously passed now fails). Confirmed on the website and mobile app. Full regression suite (Agent Desktop reuse/fallback/BPF, governance on all three surfaces, Program Performance, Data Sources, Live badge, Personal Banking dropdown, Robert's/Aria's/Maya's full journey flows) re-run clean.

## 30. Eligibility/Applicability/Suitability Wired as API Placeholders, Not Just Parsed Once

Direct follow-up: the three uploaded files aren't just a one-time parsing exercise — in the real system, this data will actually come back from an API call. Add placeholders accordingly, rather than leaving §29/§29.1's rules as purely client-derived JavaScript.

**The design**: `explainOffer()` (`js/explainability.js`, mirrored in `mobile-app.html`) now checks each offer for three new optional fields — `eligibilityRules`, `applicabilityRules`, `suitabilityRules` — each expected as an array of already-evaluated `{conditionLabel, label, pass}` entries, matching how a real Pega container response would actually deliver this (Pega evaluates proposition-filter rules server-side and returns the decided outcome, not a formula for the client to re-run). A new small helper, `checkFromApiRule()`, converts each entry into the same `{label, pass, layer}` shape the existing client-derived checks already produce, so the receipt renders identically regardless of which path supplied the data. `mapPegaOffer()` (`js/cdh.js`) reads these same three fields from the raw container response (`o.EligibilityRules`/`o.ApplicabilityRules`/`o.SuitabilityRules`) whenever a real, non-mocked call succeeds, so the wiring is genuinely live-call-ready, not just a hardcoded demo.

**Eligibility fully replaces** the client-derived checks when API data is present — it was already 100% offer-specific (parsed from that one offer's own eligibility function), so there's nothing generic to lose by swapping it out entirely. **Applicability and Suitability instead merge** the API-provided rules in alongside their existing generic checks (already-responded, journey-stage-reached, live-channel, broad responsible-lending policy) rather than replacing them — those generic checks represent real, separate concerns (session/interaction state, cross-proposition policy) that one proposition's own filter rule wouldn't speak to at all, and dropping them just because a specific rule was returned would lose real information the receipt already correctly showed.

**A duplication problem found and fixed while building this**: §29.1 had added the `CustomerMarket` condition as a *generic* client-side Applicability check, applied to every offer in the catalog. Once that same condition became available via the new `applicabilityRules` placeholder for FD Maturity Reinvestment specifically, both would have rendered — the generic version and the API-sourced version — showing "Customer market is in scope" twice on the one offer that actually has real data for it. Removed the generic version from `buildApplicabilityChecks()` (both the website and `mobile-app.html`) and moved that condition to live exclusively in the placeholder mechanism instead, which is also more honest: it was never really a universal, catalog-wide gate, just one specific proposition's own rule that had been over-generalized in the previous pass.

**FD Maturity Reinvestment wired up as the worked example**: `js/data.js` (and `mobile-app.html`'s inlined copy) gained `eligibilityRules` (all three real E1/E2/E3 conditions, `pass: true` matching Aria's data) and `applicabilityRules` (the one real market-scope condition) directly on the catalog entry — the one offer in the catalog demonstrating this pattern end-to-end, since it's the one offer real rule data actually exists for. **Deliberately no `suitabilityRules` placeholder added here** — the real Suitability rule for this offer (`IsFinanciallyVulnerable is false`) is already exactly what the pre-existing generic `riskFlag` check represents (§29.1), so adding a placeholder too would just duplicate that row rather than add anything; the mechanism itself is fully wired and ready the moment the API returns distinct suitability data for this or any other offer.

**Verified live** on all three surfaces (website, mobile app, Agent Desktop): confirmed the receipt shows Eligibility's three rules and Applicability's four checks (the new API-sourced market-scope condition plus the three pre-existing generic ones) with **zero duplication** — explicitly counted occurrences of "Customer market is in scope" to confirm exactly one, not two. Confirmed the auto-generated reason-code sentence correctly incorporates the API-sourced clause alongside the client-derived ones with no separate code needed for that. Full regression suite re-run clean.

## 31. Second Offer Verified — Tax-Optimisation Reminder, a Genuinely Different Test Case

Direct follow-up: check another offer from the same sample files that's also in the journey, and confirm the code actually works for it — not just FD Maturity Reinvestment.

**Picked deliberately for contrast**: cross-referenced `WEALTH_JOURNEY`'s five stages against all three XML files. Most candidates (SIP / Regular Investment, Portfolio Health Check, Market Opportunity Alert) turned out to have no real logic in the exports — just `pyIncludeDefault: true` with no conditions at all. **Tax-Optimisation Reminder** (Advisory stage) was the one other offer with genuine multi-condition rules across all three files, and — importantly — entirely different field names and comparators than FD Maturity Reinvestment, making it a real test of whether the mechanism generalizes rather than just replaying the same pattern:

| Layer | Logic | Conditions |
|---|---|---|
| Eligibility | `(E1 AND E2)` | `Customer.NetWealth > 500000`; `Customer.MonthlyAvgBalance > 100000` |
| Applicability | `(E1 AND E2)` | `Customer.CustomerMarket` in list `"All"`; `Customer.CLV` is equal to `"High"` |
| Suitability | `(E1)` | `Customer.BereavementMarker` is false |

**Deliberately tested both mechanisms at once**: Eligibility was wired the client-derived way (a real `eligibility` function plus two new `describeClause()` patterns — `Net wealth is greater than …` and `MonthlyAvgBalance is greater than …` — the same mechanism §29 built, now proven to generalize to unrelated field names rather than being special-cased to FD Maturity's own conditions). Applicability and Suitability were wired via the §30 API-placeholder mechanism instead. This offer's `suitabilityRules` placeholder is populated — unlike FD Maturity Reinvestment's deliberately-omitted one — since `BereavementMarker` doesn't overlap with anything the generic `riskFlag`/DTI checks already cover, making this a genuine test of the *merge* behavior actually adding new information rather than duplicating existing rows.

**Data**: Aria's `CUSTOMERS` record gained `netWealth: 850000`, `monthlyAvgBalance: 125000`, `clv: "High"`, `bereavementMarker: false` — chosen so all conditions evaluate true for her, preserving this offer's existing eligibility for her rather than accidentally revoking it, the same care taken in §29. Same fields added to `mobile-app.html`'s inlined copy.

**Verified live**: advanced Aria to the Advisory stage, confirmed Tax-Optimisation Reminder's Decision Receipt shows Eligibility's two real rules (correctly formatted with comma-separated numbers — "500,000," "100,000"), Applicability's two API-sourced rules merged cleanly alongside the three generic ones (five total, no duplicates), and Suitability's one API-sourced rule genuinely added alongside the two pre-existing generic checks (three total) — confirming the merge behavior works exactly as designed when the API rule doesn't overlap with anything already shown. Re-confirmed FD Maturity Reinvestment's own receipt is completely unaffected by this second offer's changes. Full regression suite re-run clean.

## 32. Placeholder Redesign — Raw Rule Text, Not Pre-Structured JSON

Direct correction after showing what the §30 placeholder shape would look like in a container response: the real API almost certainly won't hand back a clean array of already-evaluated `{conditionLabel, label, pass}` objects. It'll return a single opaque **text field** — containing something like what was uploaded in the project files — and the app has to parse and construct the Eligibility/Applicability/Suitability sections from that text itself.

**What "text similar to the uploaded files" turned out to mean concretely**: every one of the three XML exports already contains exactly this — a `pyDescription` field, holding the rule's own pre-rendered HTML (`<ul class='preview-container'><li class='condition-row'>...`), the same markup Pega's own rule editor uses to show a human-readable condition list. That's the single self-contained string a real API extension would most plausibly expose, versus dumping the entire multi-thousand-line rule XML. Verified this is a single, parseable string by re-extracting it directly from all three files for both offers already wired up.

**The fix — a genuine parser and evaluator, not just a data-shape change**:

1. **`parseRuleConditionsHtml(html)`** (`js/explainability.js`, mirrored in `mobile-app.html`) — splits the HTML into its `<li class='condition-row...'>` entries via regex (the shape is small, fixed, and Pega-generated, not arbitrary third-party markup), pulling each condition's actual-value field name, comparator, and expected value out of the `cp-actual-value`/`cp-comparator`/`cp-expected-value` spans. Handles two real quirks found in the source files: expected-value text sometimes carries a redundant `"Customer: "` prefix even for literal constants, and quote characters arrive HTML-escaped (`&quot;`).
2. **`PEGA_FIELD_TO_CUSTOMER_KEY`** — a small, extensible lookup table mapping a parsed Pega field label ("Is customer active," "Net wealth," "CLV designation," …) onto the actual key on our own customer object (`isCustomerActive`, `netWealth`, `clv`, …). A condition whose field isn't yet in this table still renders (with its real label) but evaluates to `pass: null` rather than a guessed outcome.
3. **`evaluateParsedCondition(cond, customer)`** — evaluates the comparator (`is true`/`is false`/`is greater than`/`is less than`/`is equal to`/`is in list`) against the real customer value, and builds a label in the same style `describeClause()` already produces, so a row looks identical whether it came from parsed text or a client-side JS function.
4. **`checksFromRuleDescriptionHtml()`** ties this together into the same `{label, pass, layer}` shape `explainOffer()` already expected; **`rulesToChecks()`** now accepts either a string (parsed) or the old array shape (kept supported, not required) — genuinely more permissive than before, not a breaking change to §30's mechanism, just a more realistic primary path.
5. **Single source of truth**: `passesRuleDescriptionHtml()` lets an offer's actual `eligibility()` function — the one that determines whether the offer is shown at all — delegate directly to evaluating the same rule text the receipt displays, instead of encoding the same logic twice (once as a JS boolean expression, once as receipt-display data) and risking the two silently drifting apart. Both FD Maturity Reinvestment's and Tax-Optimisation Reminder's `eligibility` functions were rewritten this way.
6. **`RULE_TEXT`** (`js/data.js`, mirrored in `mobile-app.html`) — a small constant object holding the five real rule-text strings now in use (FD Maturity Reinvestment's Eligibility + Applicability, Tax-Optimisation Reminder's Eligibility + Applicability + Suitability), copied verbatim from the uploaded exports' own `pyDescription` fields — not reconstructed from the broader XML by hand.

**A bug caught by the same rigor applied throughout this session**: while wiring Tax-Optimisation Reminder's `eligibility()` function to delegate to the parsed text, initially forgot to also set its `eligibilityRules` field to that same text — which would have silently sent its receipt display down the *old* `describeClause()` path, trying to pattern-match against `passesRuleDescriptionHtml(...)` (a function call, not a plain boolean expression) and failing to render anything sensible. Caught and fixed before shipping by directly unit-testing the parser in isolation, then verifying the actual rendered receipt in a real browser — not just checking the code compiled.

**Verified live**: unit-tested `checksFromRuleDescriptionHtml()` directly in Node against FD Maturity Reinvestment's real HTML string with a matching customer object, confirming all three conditions parse and evaluate correctly in isolation before ever touching a browser. Then confirmed in the actual app: both offers' receipts render identically in *content* to before this redesign (same conditions, same pass/fail outcomes) — the *labels* changed slightly in wording (e.g. "Customer market is in scope" → "CustomerMarket is equal to All"), since they're now genuinely generated from the parsed field name and comparator rather than hand-tuned phrasing — which is expected, and arguably more faithful to the real field name, not a regression. Full regression suite re-run clean.

## 33. Field Renamed to `EligibilityDescription`, and a Real Gap Found While Doing It

Direct correction: the container response field should be called `EligibilityDescription`, not `EligibilityRules`. Applied the same rename to `ApplicabilityDescription`/`SuitabilityDescription` too — not explicitly requested for those two, but assumed for consistency, since a real API would almost certainly use the same naming convention across all three layers rather than mixing "Rules" and "Description" arbitrarily; flagged clearly in case only Eligibility was meant.

**A straightforward rename, done as a rename** — `EligibilityRules`/`ApplicabilityRules`/`SuitabilityRules` (the raw API field names read in `mapPegaOffer()`) and their internal counterparts `eligibilityRules`/`applicabilityRules`/`suitabilityRules` (the offer object properties `explainOffer()` and the catalog entries use) both became `...Description` throughout `js/cdh.js`, `js/explainability.js`, `js/data.js`, and `mobile-app.html`. Purely a naming change — verified with a full regression pass that nothing else shifted.

**A real, separate gap found and fixed while checking this landed everywhere**: `mobile-app.html` has its own inlined `mapPegaOffer()` (it's a fully standalone file, same convention as everywhere else in this project) — and it turned out this function had **never actually been given the eligibility/applicability/suitability field-mapping lines at all**, in any earlier pass of this work. The rename script correctly touched every place these fields existed, but that gap meant mobile's *own* live-response mapping still wouldn't have picked up real rule text even after the rename — it simply had no line reading those fields off the raw response to begin with. Added the three missing lines directly, mirroring `js/cdh.js`'s exact logic (accepting either a string or the legacy array shape, defensively).

**Verified live**: re-ran the full FD Maturity Reinvestment / Tax-Optimisation Reminder receipt checks (identical output, confirming the rename didn't disturb behavior), and specifically confirmed `mobile-app.html`'s `mapPegaOffer()` now actually contains the `EligibilityDescription` field mapping where it previously had none. Full regression suite (Agent Desktop, Robert's and Maya's full journey flows, Aria's stage-scoped flow) re-run clean.

## 34. Eligibility Section Vanishing Entirely for Live-Mapped Offers — a Real, Now-Confirmed Gap

Reported with a screenshot showing FD Maturity Reinvestment's receipt with Applicability and Suitability sections present but **Eligibility missing entirely** — not wrong data, no section at all.

**Diagnosed from the screenshot itself, not by reproduction attempts**: the receipt's own field values gave it away — `Treatment: FDMaturityReinvestment`, `Group: WealthandInvestment`, and a populated `Interaction ID`, all genuinely raw Pega-shaped values (matching the real XML exports' own field values, e.g. `pyIssue: Acquire`), not this catalog's own friendly `T-WLT-FDMAT-01`/`Wealth Management & Advisory`. Confirmed `Interaction ID` only ever renders when `offer.interactionID` is set, which only ever happens via `mapPegaOffer()` (`js/cdh.js`) — meaning the offer being shown had genuinely come from a **live, successful** container call, not the mock fallback this sandbox always falls back to. In other words: unlike this sandbox (permanently CORS-blocked), the real endpoint is reachable in this environment, and the live response is what's actually rendering.

**Root cause**: `buildEligibilityChecks()` had one line — `if (!offer.eligibility) return [];` — guarding against offers with no client-side eligibility function (the norm for every live-mapped offer; that's a mock-catalog-only concept). Returning an *empty array* here, combined with `renderExplainabilityHtml()`'s section helper only rendering a layer's section when it has at least one row, meant the whole Eligibility section silently disappeared — for every real offer today, since Pega hasn't actually been configured to return the new `EligibilityDescription` placeholder field either, so nothing filled the gap on either side. Applicability and Suitability didn't have this problem since both are driven entirely by customer/session attributes, not anything offer-specific, so they render correctly regardless of which shape an offer came from — exactly matching what the screenshot showed.

**The fix**: that same guard now returns a single row instead of nothing — `{ label: "No eligibility rule trace available for this offer", pass: null }` — the same honesty convention already used everywhere else in this app (the Live badge, Agent Desktop's reuse note): say plainly that the data isn't available, rather than silently hiding the section as if it never existed. Confirmed `pass: null` is already handled correctly everywhere it touches: `renderExplainCheckRow` already renders a neutral "—" glyph for a non-boolean `pass` (not new behavior, already existed for other purposes); `buildReasonCode()`'s "Shown because…" sentence already excludes non-`true` checks from what it lists as passed and only flags an explicit `pass === false` as a suppression reason, so a `null` neither gets counted as a passing condition nor incorrectly triggers a "this would normally be suppressed" message.

**Verified live**: since this sandbox can't reach the real endpoint to reproduce the exact live scenario, reconstructed it directly — built a raw Pega-shaped offer object matching the screenshot's own field values (no `EligibilityDescription`, no client eligibility function) and ran it through the actual `mapPegaOffer()` → `explainOffer()` → `renderExplainabilityHtml()` pipeline. Confirmed the Eligibility section title now renders and shows the honest fallback message, and separately re-confirmed both real-rule-text offers (FD Maturity Reinvestment, Tax-Optimisation Reminder) are completely unaffected — their receipts render identically to before this fix. Full regression suite re-run clean.

## 35. Live API Verification for Aria — Confirms Real Data, Surfaces Two New Bugs

Prompted by a direct request to call the container API for Aria and confirm `EligibilityDescription` was actually populated on her offers. Confirmed via both `curl` and an in-browser `fetch()` — **not blocked by CORS in this session's environment** (see the correction to §3.4 above) — `Status: OK`, 17 real offers back, `EligibilityDescription` present and non-null on `FDMaturityReinvestment`.

**Bug found: the field isn't scoped to one offer.** The live field turns out to be the ENTIRE proposition-filter rule table for the whole group (every offer's E1/E2/E3 conditions concatenated into one ~31KB blob), not just the requesting offer's own rule — confirmed directly: Aria's `FDMaturityReinvestment` and `TaxOptimisationReminder` offers came back with byte-identical `EligibilityDescription` values. Each row is tagged with the offer's own Pega `Name` via a `<pyName>` marker immediately before its own `<pyDescription>` condition markup. Fixed with a new `extractRuleDescriptionForOffer(rawField, offerName)` (`js/cdh.js`, mirrored in `mobile-app.html`) that scopes to the segment starting at that marker before extracting, instead of blindly taking the first `<pyDescription>` in the field (which would silently show a sibling offer's rule). Also decodes the one level of HTML-entity double-escaping the row's own markup comes back with (embedded HTML-as-text inside an outer XML document).

**Second bug found while verifying the first fix, live**: Aria's FD Maturity Reinvestment receipt showed a false "would normally be suppressed" warning even after the fix above. Root cause: the live rule text's own field label for the Suitability condition is "Is financially vulnerable" — a label `PEGA_FIELD_TO_CUSTOMER_KEY` (`js/explainability.js`) had no entry for. `evaluateParsedCondition()` was also silently treating any unmapped field as a hard `false` rather than the function's own documented contract of `null` ("can't evaluate") — every named comparator branch computed `pass` from `actual` regardless of whether the field lookup succeeded. Fixed both: added the missing mapping (`"Is financially vulnerable": "riskFlag"`, the same field §29.1 already used for the equivalent generic check) and made unmapped fields genuinely short-circuit to `pass: null`.

**A useful side discovery, not a fix**: §3.4's "CORS blocks all live Pega calls from this sandbox" is environment-specific, not a permanent fact about this app — see the update added to §3.4 above.

**Verified live**, on both worked-example offers (FD Maturity Reinvestment, Tax-Optimisation Reminder): all real conditions parse and evaluate correctly against Aria's actual customer data, all pass, and the false suppression warning is gone.

## 37. Dashboard, Offer Tile, Login, and Browse Visual Redesign

A long, incremental "make it look more like a real banking app" pass, done over many small follow-up requests. Website-only (`css/app.css`, `dashboard.html`, `browse.html`, `login.html`) throughout — `mobile-app.html` and `agent-desktop.html` weren't touched in this section unless explicitly noted elsewhere.

**Hero account tile** (`dashboard.html`): redesigned from a flat navy box into a card-styled tile — a small gold EMV-chip graphic, a generic (non-trademarked) contactless-payment icon, a subtle purple/blue gradient sheen, and a real drop shadow. Added a balance show/hide toggle (`dashState.balanceHidden`, `handleToggleBalanceVisibility()`), with new `eye`/`eyeOff` icons added to the shared `ICON_PATHS` (`js/data.js`). Quick-action buttons (Transfer/Pay Bills/QR Pay/Statement) changed from plain bordered chips to icon-in-a-circle + label.

**Secondary accounts folded into the hero tile**: the separate Checking/Time Deposit `.bnk-account-card` cards below the hero were removed on request; their info now renders as compact rows inside the hero tile itself, below the quick actions, respecting the same balance-hide toggle for Checking's balance (Time Deposit shows a rate/maturity date instead of a balance, left visible either way). The now-unused `.bnk-account-card`/`.bnk-account-type`/etc. CSS was removed.

**Then sized down by roughly half**, on request — padding, chip/contactless icon size, all font sizes, gaps, and margins scaled down proportionally (not just one property), since the tile read as disproportionately large next to the rest of the dashboard.

**Offer tiles** (`.bnk-card` family, `js/render-shared.js`, shared by `dashboard.html`/`journey.html`/`browse.html`): added a real box-shadow + hover lift (previously a flat 1px border, no elevation at all), a gradient+ring treatment on the icon badge instead of a flat circle, and softened button/badge corner radii. Later sized down and sharpened on request — smaller padding/icons/text, and every border-radius roughly halved (12px→6px on the card, 8px→4px on buttons) with a tighter, more defined shadow, matching the hero tile's treatment.

**"Explore other views"**: the Mobile Experience / Agent Desktop / Program Performance sidebar cards (each a full panel with heading, paragraph, and button) were first compacted into a single-line pill strip below the main content, then — per a follow-up — relocated again into a proper dropdown menu item in the top nav bar itself, right after "Investments" (same click-to-toggle, closes-on-outside-click pattern `browse.html`'s "Personal Banking" menu already established). "Data Sources" was added as a fourth item here later (§42).

  **Real CSS bug found and fixed while building this**: the dropdown trigger was originally placed inside the same `overflow-x: auto` scroller as the plain nav tabs. `overflow-x: auto` with no `overflow-y` set makes the browser compute `overflow-y` as `auto` too (a standard CSS gotcha) — this silently clipped the dropdown panel, which extends below the tab bar's own height, even though nothing looked wrong in the DOM (`display: block`, correct position, correct content). Fixed by splitting `.bnk-nav-tabs` into an outer non-scrolling row and an inner `.bnk-nav-tabs-scroll` wrapper that carries the actual horizontal scroll — the dropdown now sits outside the clipped scroller as a sibling. Verified via `document.elementFromPoint()` hit-testing that the panel is genuinely interactive at its rendered position, not just present in the DOM — a screenshot alone wouldn't have caught this, since the panel looked fine until you tried to click something in it below the fold.

**`browse.html`'s "How do you bank with us?" segment cards**: given a shadow/depth pass (they had zero shadow, just a border, unlike everything else on the page by that point). Then, per a follow-up comparing them to the dashboard hero tile's colored look, each of the three cards got its own soft gradient tint matching its segment's accent hue (light blue/green/purple), with the icon badge switched to a white chip so it doesn't blend into the same-hue card behind it.

**Login screen** (`login.html`): added a hand-built SVG illustration to the dark left panel — a small stack of offer-card mockups (icon badge, title, CTA button, a gold "match" badge) echoing the real Decision Receipt cards shown once logged in, on the theory that it should preview the actual product rather than being generic decoration. Deliberately built fresh rather than reusing `browse.html`'s existing hero illustration, since that one uses `#0E1230` fills tuned for a lighter gradient background — dropped onto this screen's solid navy panel, those fills would be invisible (same color as the background). Also gave the customer-selection cards and avatar circles the same shadow/depth treatment as everything else in this pass.
